###### **2.2.2. JIWRF and SSWRF Wet Processes - Focus Area** 

- JIWRF and SSWRF are the backbone of MMSD’s performance, and our strategy builds on the principle that system- 

- **JIWRF &SSWRF WET PROCESSES** wide insight will drive operational excellence, linking ISS operations, plant hydraulics, and process optimization through predictive controls that enhance reliability, sustainability, and permit compliance. 

###### _Systemwide Integration for Optimized Operations_ 

Our operating approach for the WRFs’ liquid treatment trains focuses on disciplined and data-driven process control across MMSD’s entire conveyance and treatment system. We’ll coordinate flow management in the ISS and tunnel with real-time plant hydraulics to maximize throughput, prevent overloads, and achieve zero CSOs by 2035. Pump station operations will align dynamically with conveyance conditions. We’ll optimize clarifier and aeration processes in tandem to balance biological loading and solids management between JIWRF and SSWRF. 

A **centralized digital operations dashboard** integrating SCADA, CMMS, and process analytics will make hydraulics, energy use, aeration performance, and effluent quality visible to operators. This data-informed platform will support stable operations, early risk detection, and proactive decision-making: 

- **Preliminary and Primary Treatment.** Monitoring screens, grit removal, and clarifier performance will prevent headloss and ensure efficient solids capture. We’ll optimize primary sludge withdrawal rates to balance downstream biological and solids loading. 

- **Secondary (Biological) Treatment.** Jacobs will evaluate, in collaboration with MMSD, implementation of advanced DO control with real-time ammonia/nitrate monitoring and model-predictive control of aeration systems to reduce energy use while ensuring compliance. Aeration and RAS flows will be dynamically adjusted based on real-time loadings to maintain stable mixed liquor suspended solids (MLSS) concentrations, prevent secondary clarifier stress, and support responsive wet weather operations. 

- **Tertiary and Disinfection.** To ensure reliable pathogen control and readiness for future _E. coli_ permit requirements, we’ll maintain automated chlorination and dechlorination systems with feedback loops tied to flow and chlorine residuals using machine learning, in combination with MMSD’s new UV system. These automated systems will still require human operator review and approval.

###### _Specific Optimization Opportunities at SSWRF and JIWRF_ 

We’ll address two high-priority operational challenges at SSWRF and JIWRF that highlight the complexity and importance of optimizing liquid treatment processes in collaboration with MMSD to **ensure reliable performance, regulatory compliance, and cost efficiency** . At SSWRF, persistent settleability issues that have resulted in effluent violations call for solutions such as primary clarifier stabilization, selector zone optimization, and enhanced process control. At JIWRF, there’s opportunity for significant gains by improving aeration efficiency, refining channel mixing, and applying intelligent process control. Together, these approaches underscore our commitment to leveraging advanced technologies and operational expertise to deliver robust, sustainable solutions for MMSD. 

**Addressing SSWRF Settleability Issues.** Settleability at SSWRF has been an ongoing challenge for MMSD. Jacobs commends MMSD on upgrading the diffuser system and including an optional unaerated zone to select out filamentous organisms. Jacobs will partner with MMSD to maximize the benefit of these upgrades while pursuing additional targeted improvements, including those presented in **Exhibit IV-17** . 

As stated in the diffuser upgrade design, the new selector zone alone cannot guarantee a sufficient reduction in sludge volume index (SVI) to maintain treatment and prevent clarifier overloading. Beyond optimizing existing and planned components, there are opportunities at SSWRF for low-cost improvements and process intensification with short payback periods that we’ll discuss with MMSD. **Jacobs brings proven technologies and experience to help unlock these gains, including the following examples:** 

- **Hydrocyclones** . Hydrocyclones improve sludge settleability and SVI by selectively wasting lighter flocculant sludge while retaining denser granules and functional organisms (phosphorus accumulating organisms, amonia-oxidizing bacteria, nitrite-oxidizing bacteria) that stabilize biological processes. This technology is widely adopted, compatible with ongoing aeration upgrades, and requires minimal infrastructure (e.g., upgrade waste activated sludge [WAS] pumping facilities). Jacobs has implemented hydrocyclones globally with strong results. 

Jacobs will measure and report performance using dashboards and KPIs aligned with MMSD’s priorities, including effluent quality, energy use per

- **Step Feed Optimization** . Upgrading the existing step feed process to step feed biological nutrient removal (BNR) introduces flow into a downstream anoxic zone, enabling denitrification and reducing settleability impacts. This approach increases selector zone hydraulic retention time, lowers NO₃ loading in RAS, and improves phosphorus removal. **A two-pass step feed configuration could boost capacity by 10–15%.** 

- **Mobile Biofilm Activated Sludge (MBAS).** MBAS, developed by Jacobs and Nuvoda, uses mobile plant-based media integrated with MLSS to enhance treatment without major footprint expansion. Unlike fixed-film systems, MBAS media circulates with RAS and is retained and returned to the aeration basins via screening of the WAS stream. At SSWRF, MBAS could increase capacity to improve wet-weather handling, increase nitrification, improve settleability and enable biological phosphorus removal. While blower upgrades and aeration control remain necessary, **MBAS offers a cost-effective retrofit with significant performance gains. It could increase treatment capacity by up to 40% at South Shore.**

**JIWRF Aeration - Unlocking Energy and Process Gains.** JIWRF has a strong track record of meeting or exceeding permit requirements with the existing configuration. However, the amount of air used for bioreactor aeration and channel mixing **provides an opportunity to further reduce energy-related operating costs** . We’ll work with MMSD to continue improving these systems. Key dynamics impacting aeration blower efficiency at JIWRF include: 

- Big bubble channel mixing will bring new anaerobic/anoxic volume to the biological process. This may improve biological phosphorus removal but could also impact the loading on the aeration basins. 

- Dissolved oxygen (DO) variability observed across the aeration basins in service (See **Exhibit IV-18** ) indicate that individualized basin setpoints, **improved load balance, and refined airflow distribution to the aeration basins would yield substantial energy and process benefits.** 

We’ll embed these operational insights into Jacobs’ Intelligent O&M and hybrid optimizer digital tools to deliver near-term gains while informing MMSD’s long-term aeration system upgrade strategy with defensible, data-driven

**2.2.3. Optimizing JIWRF and SSWRF Solids Processing and Milorganite Production - Focus Area** MMSD has operated its solids processing and Milorganite **SOLIDS PROCESSING** production systems very effectively for almost a century, **& MILORGANITE** consistently meeting production and product-quality objectives. **PRODUCTION** Even with this strong performance, the system remains uniquely complex: sludge streams from JIWRF and SSWRF are highly strategic risk management. interdependent, and Milorganite quality and quantity depend directly on the relative sources and proportions of these solids. Balancing solids transfers through the IPS adds additional operational challenges, as these decisions influence treatment performance and energy demands across both plants. At the same time, the integrated energy system—biogas, heat recovered from engine generators, renewable landfill gas, and supplemental natural gas—presents both constraints and opportunities within the overall production process. Building on MMSD’s already strong operations, our approach offers targeted ideas to further optimize solids handling, energy use, and Milorganite production in a coordinated, systemwide manner. 

Our approach focuses on identifying process and energy optimization while maintaining clean, well-managed facilities that support sustainable solids processing and ensure reliable Milorganite production that consistently meets product specifications and Class A compliance through strategic risk management. 

**Efficient Solids Processing to Minimize Costs and Maximize Renewable Energy** . Our goal in operating the biosolids processes is to minimize cost and maximize the use of renewable energy. Through the energy plan we developed for MMSD on a previous project, we understand the nexus between biosolids processing and energy management, as illustrated in **Exhibit IV-19** . To optimize biosolids processing operations, we’ll build on past efforts and develop an integrated process and energy model that can be used to run “what-if” scenarios, so we can evaluate with MMSD opportunities such as those described below.

B. Pre-term Activities 

IV. APPROACH 

**Maximize the share of digested biosolids sent to the Milorganite process— beyond the current ~30–35% limit—while maintaining nitrogen content and product quality.** To increase digested biosolids feed, we first need to manage the factors that currently limit how much can be blended into the total Milorganite feed. The key risks are changes in pellet quality, higher chaff and dust production, and a drop in Milorganite nitrogen content. To address these issues, we propose a pilot study to evaluate replacing the existing dryer screw mixers with a more effective pug mill mixer equipped with VFD drives. We understand MMSD will soon study adding new dryers at SSWRF, and those new dryers may include improved cake mixing. However, it may be more cost-effective to upgrade mixing in the existing D&D facility now, rather than waiting for construction of a new SSWRF dryer facility. 

**Maximize the amount of landfill gas used in the JIWRF turbines and dryers while maximizing the power generation in the turbines.** Historical data shows that the generator auxiliary equipment has contributed to most of the down time for the Solar Turbines. Jacobs will work with MMSD during the transition and CMMS programming to ensure the proper PM/PdM is assigned to all the generator system assets. This will include identifying spare parts and ensuring they are onsite to minimize any power generation down time. Maximizing turbine availability will reduce natural gas use and purchased power while increasing the amount of waste heat available for drying and building heat. In the future, when systems such as auxiliary equipment fail, Jacobs will deploy our SMEs to analyze failures, implement corrective actions, and prevent repeat issues. 

**Increasing the concentration of dryer cake feed solids to reduce energy needed for drying.** The energy required to produce Milorganite could be more than half of the energy used by MMSD. We estimate that for every 1% increase in cake solids, $350K/year in purchased natural gas can be saved. In addition, increasing the cake solids would potentially increase dryer capacity, resulting in more available dryers and more reliable Milorganite production. 

MMSD, along with Jacobs team member Marty Dierker, is currently running tests on the feasibility of increasing cake dryness. Thickening and dewatering chemicals; thickened and dewatered sludge concentrations; increased chaff and dust production; pellet shape and hardness; and dryer throughput could each adversely affect cake solids and Milorganite quality. Adding a more efficient pugmill mixer, as discussed above, could help address some of these issues, but must be carefully evaluated and tested to ensure Milorganite quality is not compromised.

**Reliable Milorganite Production with Strategic Risk Management.** To help meet Milorganite production and distribution goals, we intend to collaborate with MMSD to evaluate the following opportunities to make production more reliable and consistent. ###### **Collecting Chaff from Milorganite Production in Specialized Dumpsters for** 

- **Develop detailed and effective drying and dewatering SOPs and train operators in their execution to** mitigate the risk of producing Milorganite that is not compliant with the specifications. For example, operators must be trained on the new, more robust sieve analysis. 

**Disposal in a Landfill.** We’ll work with MMSD to investigate options to minimize chaff production, which would lower disposal costs. For transport and disposal, chaff could be hauled in standard vehicles or placed in roll-off boxes using disposable filter “socks” (the same type planned for other wastes). The sock is attached to the box and filled through an extended neck that keeps the dusty material contained. The roll-off is then hauled to the landfill, where the entire filled sock is tipped and disposed. This approach helps control dust during loading, transport, and unloading. 

- **Carefully monitor silo storage capacity and work closely with MMSD and its packaging contractor to mitigate the risk of reaching silo storage capacity** during low agricultural demand seasons, ensuring dryer production does not have to be curtailed.

**Maintaining Clean Facilities** . The D&D and silo facilities are difficult to keep clean because of dust and fine particles that escape during drying, processing, and storage. We’ll develop detailed, OSHA-compliant SOPs so cleaning is performed consistently and effectively in accordance with housekeeping to manage dust as required in the Scope of Services. The D&D dust control system is aging, and leaks and plugging can worsen dust buildup. We understand MMSD has ongoing and planned capital improvements. We’ll also work with MMSD to evaluate and integrate capital projects that support long-term energy goals. To guide day-to-day decisions, we propose developing a system-wide, dynamic energy balance that builds on the “Sankey” model Jacobs developed for MMSD’s Energy Plan (see **Exhibit IV-19** above). We’ll expand the model to include solids loading, interplant transfers, turbine heat recovery, and gas distribution. The result will function as an operational dashboard across both plants. It will help operators compare options in real time—such as when to transfer sludge between plants, how to run GE turbines to manage peak demand, and how best to route landfill gas to the Solar Turbines and dryers. 

_JIWRF Solar Turbines: Targeted PdM will increase turbine uptime and renewable energy generation_ 

**JIWRF Power Generation.** JIWRF’s complex energy system relies heavily on landfill gas and natural gas to fuel the Solar and GE turbines that supply both electricity and critical waste heat for Milorganite drying—MMSD’s single largest energy demand. Jacobs’ operating strategy focuses on maximizing renewable fuel use, heat recovery, and turbine uptime while minimizing demand charges as such: 

- **Enhance demand management** , including coordinated load shedding and exploring short-duration aeration adjustments where appropriate and compliant. 

- � **Maximize the use of available landfill gas** , directing it to the turbines whenever possible, and utilizing any excess in the four dual-fuel dryer burners. 

Together, these strategies will **increase renewable heat utilization, reduce purchased power, and improve resilience of the drying process** . 

- **Maximize Solar Turbine availability** through targeted PM/PdM, compressorsystem reliability improvements, and optimized landfill gas moisture control (including evaluating potential gas conditioning enhancements such as upstream chilling). 

- **Use waste heat efficiently** , prioritizing waste heat use first in the dryers, then the waste heat boiler, while minimizing waste heat blown off for pressure control. 

- **Operate the GE turbine strategically** to mitigate peak electrical demand, while coordinating with MMSD on future Solar Turbine expansion based on current feasibility studies.

**Energy Monitoring and Optimization.** Energy is one of MMSD’s most significant shared-cost items and a key metric for sustainability. Jacobs will implement a comprehensive monitoring and analytics program using SCADA data, power meters, and Jacobs’ Intelligent O&M tools ( **AI-powered analytics)** to: 

- Track daily and cumulative energy consumption by facility (depending on power meter availability), process area, and major equipment such as aeration blowers, pumping stations, sludge dryers, and dewatering systems. 

- Identify the most energy-intensive processes and recommend operational adjustments to reduce peak-demand charges and overall usage. 

- Forecast future energy demand using Jacobs’ Intelligent O&M and recommend control adjustment guidance to operators in real time to lower total energy costs without compromising performance. 

- Continue MMSD’s efforts to reduce both energy usage and demand charges while maintaining full compliance.

At comparable large-scale utilities, this approach has provided **20% energy savings** and will help MMSD meet its 2035 Vision target of 80% renewable-energy use from internal sources and 100% renewable from all sources. MMSD will receive monthly variance reports and optimization recommendations through the Energy Purchasing Committee. 

**Energy Demand Management.** Power bills contain two dominant cost components: energy usage measured in kWhrs, and energy demand measured in kW. Both are worth investigating to conserve power, but as noted earlier, the biggest energy usage at both sites (aeration blowers) must be carefully managed to ensure the contractual effluent performance targets are met. The highest peak demands at JIWRF are due to the inline tunnel pumps. **Jacobs’ analysis shows that energy demand can be managed more efficiently, with up to a 10% improvement in demand-related charges at both plants, valued at up to $600K per year in savings.** These savings can be achieved by providing predictive guidance to operations staff on the optimal times to operate major energy-intensive equipment such as the inline tunnel pumps. 

MMSD already manages this with good results, but **our analysis of historical records shows opportunities for further improvement.** Implementation is simple and requires no capital investment. Operators and managers would receive advisory push-notifications that indicate when adjusting major equipment would reduce demand charges, while staying within regulatory and operational limits. Recommendations account for rainfall, tunnel storage volumes, current and projected loading, and other plant conditions. Because these decisions rely on large amounts of data and many system variables, they are well-suited to Jacobs’ data-science tools. **This approach reduces operational risk by supporting a complex decision process currently handled by only a few individuals** and **could save hundreds of thousands of dollars each year** . 

Like all our Intelligent O&M tools, this approach is grounded in actual MMSD historical performance and established best practices—not theoretical models. All recommendations preserve operator authority and ensure no direct SCADA logic control. In essence, the system provides “driving directions” to licensed operators rather than attempting to operate equipment autonomously. 

Through disciplined operations, digital insight, predictive analytics, and strong alignment with MMSD’s capital planning, **Jacobs will transform energy management from a facility-by-facility operation into a systemwide optimization platform** . This approach supports MMSD’s 2035 Vision, reduces costs, increases renewable utilization, improves reliability of critical assets, and ensures that process stability and compliance remain at the forefront of every operating decision.

###### Specific chemical optimizations for the MMSD facilities include: 

**Sodium hypochlorite for disinfection.** Both WRFs use a significant amount of this chemical to ensure bacteria deactivation of the final effluent. We understand that UV upgrades are being planned and the usage profile for hypo will be changing. However, **our analysis of 2 years of laboratory and SCADA Historian records reveals substantial savings opportunities for the current configuration, including over 30% at JIWRF, and about 20% for the SSWRF.** While these savings values may appear unrealistic, these are _typical_ savings for this particular use case. Until now, operators have lacked predictive insights into optimal dosage rates and have relied on overly conservative dosage rates to ensure bacterial kill. This is especially true where bacteria testing involves days of lag time before laboratory results are available. In most instances, we see operators resorting to a “set it and forget it” chemical dosing approach. By contrast, we are leveraging the vast history of what dosage rates always achieved the needed fecal coliform deactivation under a wide range of operating conditions, and we can watch for those same conditions (flow, temperature, pH, time of year, etc.), as shown in **Exhibit IV-20** , to recommend the right dosage rate to apply ahead of time for operators. **Exhibit IV-21** highlights significant sodium hypochlorite savings for MMSD that could have been achieved over the past 3 years while ensuring permit and contract compliance.

The frequency and timing of recommendations is up to the operations team but varies from three to eight notifications per day. **Exhibit IV-22** provides adoption rates of dosage recommendations. 

**Sodium hypochlorite for filament control.** The SSWRF uses RAS chlorination to control filamentous bacterial growth because filaments tend to reduce settling efficiency in the secondary clarifiers, which may ultimately impact effluent water quality and regulatory compliance. Because real time information about filament populations is not available, we can use data science methods to look at what has historically driven high filament populations at the SSWRF—SRT, flows, time of year, etc. to guide when and at what dose RAS 

chlorination would be most cost effective and yield the best settling performance. Then we watch in real time for those conditions and make the matching dosage recommendations. Plus, we can continue to refine the analysis with ongoing microscopy as it becomes available to build even more accuracy into the predictions. The concept is very much like for disinfection, where real time bacteria data is not available, and residual levels are at best an after-the-fact surrogate for bacteria kill. Here, we have lagging filament data but have tremendous historical records of what is known to work best instead. **Our pricing includes savings of about 14%, or $60K per year at current chemical prices, for this use case.**

**Ferric chloride (oxidized pickle liquor) for total phosphorus control.** Another SSWRF efficiency opportunity focuses on ferric (or ferrous) chloride injection ahead of the primary clarifiers. Jacobs is aware that chemicals are currently added primarily to reduce organic loading to the secondary treatment process. As CEPT is improved at the primaries via well-mixed polymer addition (a process that Jacobs has significant experience operating) and primary BOD capture increases and stabilizes, phosphorus removal will become more of a driver for metal salt addition. Jacobs’ process simulation analysis shows that metal salt dosage is required to achieve the contractual performance standards and total phosphorus permit limits. There are two optimization methods planned: a passive change to the injection points for greater mixing and dosage efficiency, and an active management step based on data science and machine learning methods. Currently, ferric chloride is injected at each of the 48” diameter lines to each bank of primary clarifiers. We recommend the addition of a second dosage point at the effluent mixed liquor channel feeding the secondary clarifiers before a new mechanical mixer. **The benefit of this is more efficient chemical phosphorus removal (20% savings, $600K / year).** This is due to more efficient metal salt to phosphorus molar ratio, improved mixing intensity, and longer residence time. Secondary dosage will provide a backup system should the facility implement EBPR and may improve settleability. This improvement will be supported by our data science tools, as illustrated in **Exhibit IV-23** , to monitor against baseline performance and relevant water quality targets. Some additional optimization may be possible once an operating history with the multiple injection points has been established. 

We noted that a Hach Phosphax instrument to measure phosphorus content was installed but was curiously not running. We plan on using this instrument and others to further refine our machine learning approach, which may also yield more savings. 

**Future chemical optimization opportunities.** While the use cases above **conservatively total over $1M per year in savings (almost double this amount is a distinct possibility)** , there are other use cases that we’ll evaluate in the future. While insufficient data is available currently to price in the savings, things like odor control chemicals and dewatering polymer are potential targets that we have successfully optimized elsewhere.

###### **2.2.6. Odor Control and Responding to Odor Complaints - Focus Area** 

Like many wastewater utilities, MMSD’s facilities have become more susceptible to odor complaints, largely due **ODOR** to nearby residential development such as in the Third **CONTROL** Ward or in the neighborhoods around SSWRF. In addition, the proximity of large crowd events at Summerfest can result in complaints. Odors from collection system sources can be challenging to control because of the numerous emission points such as the Beach Road and Port Washington pumps stations and diversion structures, many of which are located close to residents. While MMSD has made good progress mitigating odors, remaining odorous emissions and complaints stem mainly from uncovered/untreated EXHIBIT IV-24. WATS MODEL processes such as primary clarifiers. Intermittent odor mitigation methods such as ferric chloride addition are used when complaints occur. 

- Proactively minimize odors through rigorous maintenance and monitoring 

- � Mitigate odors rapidly and effectively if they do occur 

**Proactive Odor Prevention and Systemwide Insight.** Jacobs’ odor management strategy begins with understanding and anticipating odor-forming conditions before emissions occur. Because many odor issues begin in the collection system through H₂S formation, understanding the fundamentals of that process is essential for preventing odors and mitigating them if they occur. ###### IV. APPROACH 



the top global research team on collection system sulfide modeling at Aalborg University (Denmark). The University developed the WATS Model, which predicts sulfide formation and corrosion in wastewater collection systems and can be used to evaluate odor and corrosion mitigation options. Under MMSD’s prior odor study, Jacobs built a systemwide WATS collection system model that identified odor- and corrosion-prone segments by integrating historical H₂S measurements, corrosion data, and complaint patterns, shown in **Exhibit IV-24** . 

This model allows operators to evaluate how operational changes such as adjusting pumping rates, altering flow routing, or receiving higher-strength industrial loads may increase odor or corrosion risks before they occur. When elevated risk is predicted, Jacobs prepares targeted mitigation strategies, including chemical addition, temporary vapor-phase treatment, or public notifications. We have successfully implemented these approaches for other utilities such as Oakland County (Detroit area), where Jacobs’ Milwaukee staff recommended and deployed a temporary ferric chloride dosing system to control odor-generating sulfides during critical operating periods. There may be discharges to the conveyance system that need to be pretreated or eliminated, which could be contributing to odor issues. Also, the long collection system leading to SSWRF can lead to septic conditions. Another example of the cause of MMSD odors is ISS pumping through the SSWRF force main to 6th and Oklahoma. 

_Temporary Ferric Chloride Odor Control System for Oakland County Michigan was installed in 2 weeks to mitigate collection system complaints_ 

###### **Installation of “Odor Early Warning System” at the WRFs to Minimize Odors** 

**Before They Occur.** Sulfides generated in the collection system will reach the treatment plants and under certain operating and meteorological conditions result in offsite odors and complaints. In addition, plant conditions such as elevated primary clarifier blanket levels or extended sludge storage times can increase sulfide generation. Currently ferric chloride is added to influent wastewater to precipitate sulfide and mitigate odors after offsite impacts are detected. **Rather than reacting after odors occur, we propose an odor early warning system** ( **Exhibit IV-25** ) that **identifies odor potential in advance, allowing operators to take proactive actions to prevent or minimize emissions** . The proposed system includes the following components: 

###### EXHIBIT IV-25.EARLY WARNING SYSTEM ALLOWS FOR PROACTIVE  EARLY WARNING SYSTEM ALLOWS FOR PROACTIVE ODOR MITIGATION MEASURES TO  ODOR MITIGATION MEASURES TO **MINIM** IZE COMPLAINTSIZE COMPLAINTS 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
Potential<br>AERMOD<br>Sulfilogger Sumo Model Complaint<br>Dispersion Model<br>Warning<br>AERMOD Dispersion Model Output<br><!-- End of picture text -->

- **Sulfiloggers™ for influent sulfide monitoring** . Sulfiloggers are an emerging technology that measure dissolved sulfides in the liquid phase upstream of the plant rather than H2S in the air as measured by traditional Odalogs. By measuring sulfides in liquid rather than the air, sulfide mass loading in the influent can be calculated and used in a control loop to automatically adjust chemical dosing. This approach accounts for highly dynamic, diurnal sulfide variations, reduces chemical costs, and avoids underdosing during peak sulfide conditions that can occur with constant-dose strategies.

###### IV. APPROACH 

� **Predictive odor modeling and community impact assessment.** While influent sulfide mass provides critical process insight, it does not indicate if odors will reach nearby receptors at concentrations that could cause complaints. To address this, we propose installing a system that will continuously estimate odor concentrations at locations surrounding the WRFs. These emissions will then be used in an AERMOD dispersion model along with real-time wind speed and direction data from a Jacobs-installed meteorological station. The model results will be displayed graphically to illustrate the shape and extent of odor plumes and identify potential offsite areas impacted. 

� When modeling indicates a risk of offsite odor impacts, notifications can be sent to residents, alderpersons, and neighborhood associations using multiple communication channels, including social media, email, and opt-in text messages. While we recognize the inherent uncertainties associated with modeling, our experience shows that with proper design and calibration, this integrated system provides a reasonably reliable early warning that is often a strong indicator of conditions likely to result in odor complaints. **Exhibit IV-26** illustrates how we calibrated our ATS model.

**Disciplined Plant Operations and Equipment Performance.** Jacobs will operate treatment processes with practices designed to minimize sulfide generation and prevent fugitive emissions, including maintaining appropriate sludge blanket depths, optimizing sludge storage durations, and closely monitoring primary and solids-handling conditions during warm weather. We understand that there may be a need under some circumstances to operate with high blankets, but operators will be trained to more closely monitor for odors when blankets are high. Operations of all covered processes will follow SOPs that ensure negative pressure is maintained, covers are secure, gasketed, and intact, and fans and ventilation systems are functioning as designed. We’ll provide ISO-aligned PM and testing for odor control units—carbon, chemical scrubbers, blowers—to maintain performance and extend media life. If ventilation systems are not operating properly, leaks can occur, which can be a significant source of odors.

###### IV. APPROACH 

**Clear, Timely, Documented Response to Public Complaints.** While we believe our proactive approach described above will decrease complaints, there still will be complaints until all odorous sources are covered and treated. **Jacobs will implement a proactive, consistent, well-documented odor complaint protocol that exceeds MMSD’s Scope of Services requirements.** Our approach integrates prevention, rapid response, and community engagement—ensuring that odor issues are minimized, investigated promptly, and communicated effectively to both MMSD and the public. Complaints received through MMSD systems, Jacobs’ website, hotlines, or social media will be logged into a centralized database with location and observed conditions. Trained staff—validated through odor sensitivity testing—will investigate each complaint using onsite observations, low-level H₂S meters, and data from the odor early warning system. We’ll assess root causes and implement corrective action when appropriate. Each complaint will generate a documented summary with findings, mitigation steps, and lessons learned, which we’ll leverage to drive continuous improvements. Residents will receive courteous follow-up, and we’ll review trends with MMSD to refine procedures and identify systemic improvements. A preliminary outline of the protocol approach is shown in **Exhibit IV-27** .

- **<mark>02</mark>**<sup>**LOGGING DETAILS**</sup> Complaint observations (odor  characteristics, weather/wind, etc.) entered into database with location 

**Continuous Improvements to Improve Community Outreach and Confidence.** Odor control performance, complaint trends, and system data will be evaluated through MMSD/Jacobs governance meetings, annual odor reviews, and dashboard reporting. Jacobs will apply insights from the WATS model, early warning analytics, SCADA trends, and operator feedback to continuously improve odor mitigation strategies and prevent recurrences. In addition to proactive operations, we’ll maintain a visible community presence, overseen by our Deputy Project Manager Rick Warner, by participating in local meetings, events, and neighborhood associations to conduct outreach, increase community confidence, and ensure that residents clearly know who to contact if concerns arise. We’ll prepare quarterly odor reports that can be shared with the public. This proactive, transparent approach strengthens regulatory compliance, reduces community impacts, and ensures MMSD maintains its role as a responsible steward of public health and neighborhood quality of life.

**Deep domain expertise powered by advanced digital tools** 

###### **2.3.1. Digital Twin: Enabling System-Wide Optimization** 

At the core of our approach is giving operators clear, actionable insights while giving MMSD full visibility into what we see, decide, and do. Jacobs’ Digital One Water platform turns system data into decision-ready information so operators can manage wet-weather 374_007CAM_1 flows, optimize treatment processes, sustain reliable Milorganite production, and anticipate equipment issues before they occur. 

A key component of our toolkit is a digital twin, which provides a dynamic, realtime model of MMSD’s infrastructure. It enables holistic management under varying conditions and supports adaptive operational decision-making, continuous compliance improvement, optimization of energy, nutrient removal, resource recovery, and strategic CSO mitigation. The digital twin does not automate plant operations. It elevates situational awareness by allowing MMSD and Jacobs to evaluate operational scenarios together and respond proactively to changing conditions. **Exhibit IV-29** presents the benefits of Jacobs’ digital twin to MMSD. 

Real-time monitoring, PdM, and integrated controls will help the teams quickly understand current conditions, identify root causes, and take timely action. These tools don’t replace operator judgment—they reinforce it by pairing real-time analytics and machine learning with proven operating experience to support proactive decision-making.

**Enhanced Dry Weather Operations.** 

Can guide our tunnel use with real-time data for dry weather load normalization and flow distribution between JIWRF and SSWRF to maximize nutrient removal efficiency, ensuring compliance across both facilities.

Helps us balance raw WAS and digested sludge that feeds thermal dryers at JIWRF, maintaining Milorganite quality while enhancing energy recovery from landfill, waste dryer heat, and digestion biogas. 

###### **Regulatory** 

**Compliance Assurance.** Helps ensure compliance by forecasting effluent quality under future conditions and recommending adjustments needed to meet requirements, reducing the risk of fines and penalties.

Allows MMSD to demonstrate system performance and improvement initiatives to internal and external stakeholders, including regulatory bodies and the public, which fosters trust and facilitates collaboration on sustainability goals.

**Optimized Wet Weather Management.** 

Anticipates and simulates wet weather events to help evaluate strategies for ISS emptying while keeping plant effluents in compliance and minimizing CSOs in the interceptor system. For example, operators decide when to start emptying the tunnel and how to split flow between the two plants by accounting for each plant’s different sludge settling behavior, thereby reducing final clarifier sludge blanket upsets—the driver of recent compliance issues. 

**Enhanced Predictive Maintenance.** 

Monitors the health of assets in real-time (e.g., pumps, pipelines, sensors, and treatment systems), identifying potential failures before they occur to minimizing downtime, reduce repair costs, and extend MMSD asset lifespans. 

**Emergency Preparedness.** 

Provides a critical tool for decision-making during extreme weather events or emergencies by simulating responses to possible floods or system surges and enabling proactive measures to mitigate risks. 

**Data Integration Capacity Monitoring and Analysis. and Trend Analysis.** 

Integrates data from various sources—SCADA systems, laboratory measurements, sensors, and historical records—into a unified platform that simplifies data analysis, enabling a deeper understanding of system behavior over time. 

Identifies patterns in flow rates, treatment performance, and storage usage by continuously analyzing data from the collection system, deep tunnel, and treatment plants and then forecasts when existing infrastructure may reach its capacity limits, providing early warnings for potential bottlenecks.

**2.4.1. Jacobs OT Asset Management Software Platform** 

MMSD increasingly relies on advanced automation and control systems to operate its treatment and conveyance network. These OT assets—analytical instruments, PLCs, networked motor controls, HMIs, and field communications—are missioncritical, yet traditional CMMS platforms aren’t designed to manage their complexity, interdependencies, and rapid configuration change. 

Jacobs will close this gap with a comprehensive OT asset management platform tailored to MMSD’s needs. Our system captures the full OT environment, from major components down to connectors, cabling, firmware, and configuration files, so teams can see asset condition, configuration health, and lifecycle risk in one place. 

This end-to-end visibility strengthens resiliency by enabling smarter decisions, proactive maintenance, and a clearer understanding of how each OT component supports critical treatment and conveyance processes, as summarized in **Exhibit IV-31** .

###### **2.4.2. Jacobs OT Asset Automated Backup and Version Control** 

To improve reliability and strengthen compliance across MMSD’s automation and controls systems, we’ll implement an automated solution for PLC backup, version control, and change management. The system will continuously save controller programs and configuration files so a verified, trusted baseline is always available for rapid recovery after a hardware failure or other unexpected change occurs, as summarized in **Exhibit IV-32** . 

Every update will be automatically tracked, creating a clear record of what changed, when it changed, and who made the change. That visibility will speed troubleshooting, support root-cause analysis, and produce an audit-ready history for regulatory and internal requirements. 

Built-in approval workflows will require review and sign-off as part of governance before changes are deployed, and automated validation checks will confirm compatibility and prevent errors. The platform will also identify “configuration drift” (when settings change from the approved baseline) and will alert staff to unauthorized edits, supporting a stronger cybersecurity posture. 

Designed to support multi-vendor environments, the solution will cover the PLCs, HMIs, drives, and gateways commonly used in water and wastewater facilities. Integrated with our OT asset management approach, each device’s logic and configuration history will be linked to the asset itself, providing full context for decision-making during maintenance, upgrades, or incident response. **The result will be less downtime, higher system integrity, and consistent standards across MMSD facilities.**

###### **2.4.3. Jacobs Managed OT Privileged Access Management (PAM) Service** 

To further strengthen cybersecurity and operational integrity, we can implement a PAM solution purpose-built for OT, as summarized in **Exhibit IV-33** , should MMSD desire. Unlike traditional VPN and jump host access—where users may gain broad network entry and passwords often remain static—PAM provides **tight, role-based access** to specific devices and applications without exposing the underlying network. 

Our PAM solution stores privileged credentials in a secure vault and automatically rotates them, eliminating the risk of password reuse and reducing the number of ways an attacker could gain access. It also manages and logs privileged sessions so activity can be monitored and recorded, creating a complete audit trail to support compliance and incident response. 

For MMSD, this would mean that a contractor who needs to update a PLC program can be granted time-limited access to that single PLC, but not to unrelated systems. With this kind of “just-in-time” access, elevated privileges exist only for the duration of approved tasks and then expire automatically. 

Integrated with our OT asset management and change control processes, PAM can provide a secure, streamlined way to manage remote and onsite privileged access, improving accountability, reducing cybersecurity risk, and eliminating the complexity and vulnerabilities of legacy remote access technologies. 



###### EXHIBIT IV-33. 

MANAGED OT PRIVILEGED ACCESS MANAGEMENT 



<!-- Start of picture text -->
303_007CAM_3<br><!-- End of picture text -->

**Granular, Role-Based Access –** System enforces precise, role-based permissions reducing cybersecurity risk. 

**Credential Vaulting and Rotation –** System stores credentials in a secure vault and rotates them automatically reducing the likelihood of unauthorized access. 

**Session Monitoring and Audit Trails –** Complete audit trail for compliance and incident response ensuring transparency and accountability for operational changes. 

**Just-in-Time Access –** Solution provides permissions only for the duration of an approved task, reducing persistent risk. 

For MMSD, this means a contractor performing maintenance on a single PLC at JIWRF can be granted access only to that controller-without exposing the rest of the process networks significantly reducing cybersecurity risk. 

Static passwords are a common vulnerability in legacy remote access solutions. For MMSD, our approach ensures that passwords for critical assets like SCADA servers or network switches are never reused or exposed, reducing the risk of credential compromise. 

Every privileged session is recorded and monitored in real time. If a technician modifies pump control logic during a wet weather event, MMSD can review the exact steps taken, ensuring transparency and accountability for operational changes. 

For MMSD, this means that when an engineer needs to troubleshoot a remote conveyance site, access is automatically revoked once the work is complete, reducing persistent risk. Traditional VPNs often leave elevated privileges active indefinitely, increasing exposure.

SECTION 2.6 

###### **2.6. Monitoring, Tracking, and Reporting Operational Parameters** 

**Monitoring,** Jacobs will deliver a comprehensive, data-driven **tracking and** framework for monitoring, tracking, and reporting **reporting of** operational parameters across MMSD’s wastewater **operational** system. Our approach focuses on transparency, **parameters** actionable insights, and continuous improvement through validated, real-time data and analytics. 

The system will continuously refine dosing strategies for hypochlorite, ferric chloride, polymer, bisulfite, and other key chemicals to maintain process stability and minimize waste. Monthly reports will document usage, cost, and correlation to process conditions, with analytics identifying opportunities for chemical-use reductions of 10 to 30% without affecting permit compliance. 

###### **2.6.3. Tunnel System Operations** 

In partnership with MMSD, Jacobs will align monitoring with the **Standards of Performance** in Exhibit C and jointly establish KPIs for shared-cost items such as energy, chemicals, and biosolids. Using data from **SCADA, CMMS, Hach WIMS, NEXGEN, and financial systems, Jacobs will generate unified dashboards accessible to both MMSD and Jacobs** . These dashboards provide a single, defensible view of our performance, linking process control, cost management, and compliance. 

MMSD’s ISS plays a vital role in controlling CSOs and maintaining system reliability. **Jacobs will integrate ISS performance parameters into the broader operational monitoring framework.** The system will continuously monitor tunnel water levels, pump station flows, and gate positions through **SCADA integration** . Predictive modeling will assess **hydraulic performance and storage utilization** , using weather forecast data to optimize system operations during wet-weather events. Real-time tracking of energy use and runtime for tunnel dewatering pumps will provide visibility into one of the largest contributors to shared power costs. After each event, Jacobs will prepare post-event reports summarizing captured volume, pump-out rate, overflow prevention, and energy utilization. Tunnel operations data will also feed into the **digital twin model** to evaluate alternative control strategies and support long-term optimization. This integrated approach will enable coordinated decision-making across treatment plants, conveyance, and storage, maximizing CSO capture efficiency while minimizing cost and energy use. 

Jacobs will configure its Intelligent O&M platform to monitor all Exhibit C parameters, including effluent quality, solids handling, and conveyance reliability. Real-time dashboards will display validated data from all source systems, while alarms and KPI exceptions will automatically generate corrective-action logs. Monthly joint reviews will evaluate efficiency, reliability, and compliance trends, and all sampling and laboratory data will maintain full traceability for permit documentation. 

###### **2.6.1. Integrated Performance Management Framework** 

Jacobs’ performance monitoring program unifies **process, maintenance, and cost data within a single analytics framework** . The **Intelligent O&M** platform, successfully deployed at systems such as in Jackson, MS, and Wilmington, DE, provides continuous data validation, analytics, and performance visualization through automated workflows and machine learning. This framework supports compliance tracking, performance measurement, and fulfillment of the Annual Settlement requirements in Article 61 of the Service Agreement. 

###### **2.6.2. Chemical Usage Monitoring and Control** 

Jacobs will track **chemical feed and consumption** to support efficient operations and accurate cost allocation. Automated metering, CMMS integration, and the Intelligent O&M chemical optimization module will record real-time dosing rates, flow-proportional usage, and cumulative consumption by process and day. Deliveries and inventory will be reconciled against meter data and purchase records. 

###### **2.6.4. Milorganite Production and Biosolids Operations** 

Jacobs will apply the same level of precision and transparency to biosolids and Milorganite production as to liquid treatment operations. Monitoring will include **solids feed rates, dewatering efficiency, polymer use, dryer energy demand, and production output** by shift and day. We’ll use dryer and furnace data already integrated into SCADA for continuous monitoring of temperature, airflow, emissions controls, and product quality. 

We’ll routinely review product temperature trends monitored and archived for **EPA 503 compliance** to confirm compliance while identifying opportunities to **improve energy efficiency and operational consistency** . Monthly reports will summarize production, energy use, chemical inputs, and product output, supporting reconciliation, planning, and sustainability objectives. We provide similar reporting for JXN Water, as described in **Exhibit IV-36** .

###### **2.6.5. Shared-Cost Transparency and Reporting** 

Jacobs will maintain a complete, auditable record of shared-cost items, including energy, chemicals, fuel, and biosolids handling, and will provide detailed reporting that supports collaborative cost management with MMSD. **Using integrated dashboards, Jacobs and MMSD will jointly review data, KPI trends, and sharedcost metrics during regular governance meetings.** All monitored parameters will align with MMSD’s **Standards of Performance** in Exhibit C, ensuring comprehensive visibility into treatment, conveyance, and solids operations as well as the cost and efficiency drivers behind them. 

Jacobs will automatically flag alarm conditions or KPI deviations and document corrective actions in a shared log visible to MMSD. Any process condition that could threaten permit compliance will trigger immediate notification to MMSD and a written summary of corrective steps. Daily operations logs and monthly performance reports will summarize flow, loading, power, chemical, and solids data. We’ll reconcile monthly shared-cost reports with invoices, inventory, and consumption data. Quarterly and annual performance summaries will be prepared for **Operations and Maintenance Committee** review, including efficiency trends, performance indicators, and improvement actions. An **Annual Performance and Optimization Report** will consolidate operational parameters, KPIs, and shared-cost metrics. All reports will be generated from validated datasets in compliance with RFP Exhibits B and Q and will be available to MMSD through secure dashboards.

Compliance Execution: Tracking, Reporting, and Dashboards** Jacobs will maintain a transparent and auditable system that consolidates operational, laboratory, and maintenance data into one defensible record. Using **Hach WIMS** integrated with MMSD’s **SCADA** and **CMMS** platforms, compliance data will be validated daily, trended monthly, and summarized quarterly for joint review. 

Dashboards will visualize permit performance, approaching limits, and parameter trends while linking results to process or maintenance events. Every report, including discharge monitoring reports, compliance maintenance annual reports, pretreatment program summaries, and air-permit documentation, will be prepared in MMSD’s established formats and delivered 100% on time and in compliance. 

All calibration records, chain-of-custody forms, and documentation will be managed through a secure, SharePoint-based electronic document management system accessible to MMSD and Jacobs staff. 

###### **2.7.2. Governance and Compliance Team** 

An **Environmental Manager** will lead permit compliance, supported by operations and laboratory QA/QC leads and Jacobs’ national **compliance and reporting team** . We commit to maintaining full compliance with all requirements included in applicable federal, state, and local regulations, including WPDES and air permits. Where MMSD’s Standards of Performance exceed minimum regulatory obligations, Jacobs affirms that those higher performance standards shall govern. We’ll consistently meet or surpass all regulatory requirements while aligning with the MMSD’s environmental stewardship. 

Jacobs will promptly notify MMSD of any conditions requiring attention and will engage regional or corporate resources as needed to support on-site staff. Compliance reporting SMEs will assist with reporting, audits, and regulatory coordination, and maintaining strong relationships with regulators to ensure transparency and advocacy on behalf of MMSD. 

Training under this program will include WPDES permit compliance, biosolids, pretreatment, and laboratory data integrity. It will be delivered through a combination of onsite and web-based modules. All training completion and competencies will be documented and shared with MMSD to verify readiness and accountability.

###### **2.7.6. Wet Weather Coordination and System Modeling** 

Jacobs will use real-time hydraulic and treatment-performance data to coordinate wet-weather operations across the ISS, conveyance system, and both WRFs. Monitoring tunnel capacity, pump back timing, and plant loading will allow operators to manage flows effectively, reduce the risk of combined sewer overflows, and maintain high treatment efficiency during storm events. 

###### **2.7.7. Cross-Facility Process Optimization** 

Jacobs will align operating strategies between JIWRF and SSWRF to improve process stability, chemical efficiency, and energy use. This includes coordinating treatment strategies, sharing process knowledge, and applying predictive tools that identify developing process issues before they affect effluent quality. 

###### **2.7.8. Knowledge Sharing and Operational Consistency** 

Operations and maintenance practices will be documented within a shared knowledge framework that captures SOP updates, lessons learned, and systemwide best practices. This promotes consistent operations between facilities and supports staff development and cross-training.

###### 3. APPROACH TO PM AND CM 

###### **3.1. Jacobs Understands and Supports MMSD’s Maintenance Objectives and Challenges** 

Although facilities appear to be in good physical condition, with some noticeable recent housekeeping, our review suggests that maintenance activities aren’t well-documented or visible to MMSD, which limits data and analysis to inform asset management decisions. Given the age and complexity of legacy and newer infrastructure, MMSD needs more than confirmation that assets run. Rather, MMSD needs an integrated and accessible asset registry complete with key attributes, maintenance records, condition data, criticality scores, and risk scores to support long-term, risk-based investment decisions.

**MMSD has asked for a different model—one that delivers proof of work and verifiable outcomes through clear, auditable documentation.** We recognize the Draft Service Agreement’s intent: execute needed work and support authorization and payment with CMMS-based records and supporting documentation. This aligns with how Jacobs runs maintenance programs every day: closed-loop planning, execution, and reporting so MMSD has decision-grade visibility into what was done, why it was needed, and what risk it mitigated. This model advances the One Water/One Vision and avoids deferred maintenance practices that can introduce operational risk. 

Our approach starts with safety and regulatory compliance—they set the maintenance standard, priorities, and urgency. We won’t “wait for thresholds” or visible deterioration to justify preventive work; we’ll plan and execute PM/PdM as a disciplined, front-end investment to prevent failures, avoid unplanned outages, and reduce the likelihood of compliance-impacting events. We’ll document every work order with field evidence (photos, parts usage, meter readings), supervisor verification, and timely closeout. Real-time dashboards—shared with MMSD—to show PM compliance, CM backlog, labor, cost, and asset health, so no work goes unverified and no trend goes unseen. In addition, our team is experienced in tracking and reporting data in NEXGEN and supporting CMMS configuration improvements, both of which will be core to the maintenance program. Finally, we’ll proactively remove a known execution barrier raised by labor—parts availability—by strengthening material readiness (critical spares, min/max levels, kitting/staging, and tighter integration between planning and storeroom/ procurement) so planned maintenance does not slip into reactive work. 

This foundation creates the **line of sight** MMSD wants, as illustrated in **Exhibit IV-41** . Governance bodies (described in **_Section II- Statement on Staffing, Training, and Corporate Culture_** ) will use this single source of truth to adjust tactics and investment priorities.

###### **3.2.1. Our ISO-aligned, 10-Box AMS is tailored to MMSD** 

We’ll tailor **Jacobs’ proven 10-Box AMS framework (Exhibit IV-43)** —based on the IAM 10-Box Excellence Model and consistent with Global Forum on Maintenance and Asset Management guidance—to MMSD’s scope and priorities. Each element is mapped to concrete deliverables, owners, and committee touchpoints so the program is auditable and durable. The collaboration with MMSD and the governance committees will drive outcomes from the AMS. 

###### <mark>1. Leadership & Governance</mark> 

**Establish an Asset Management Policy and Maintenance Governance Charter** that define objectives, decision rights, and change control. The Maintenance Committee (with CMMS and SCADA subcommittees) will be the forum for approving PM list changes, data standards, integrations, and exceptions. Outcomes include clear accountability, faster decisions, and one source of truth. 

###### <mark>2. Strategy & Planning</mark> 

**Publish the SAMP, which will include measurable objectives** for reliability, risk, safety, and cost. **Complete a baseline condition and criticality assessment** across WRF and conveyance assets; produce a risk-ranked register that drives the Long-Term Maintenance Plan and informs capital improvement planning (see No. 7 ). Tie staffing plans and materials strategy to asset risk and workload. 

###### <mark>3. Asset Management Decision-Making</mark> 

**Use risk, criticality, and lifecycle cost to pick the right action at the right time** —PM, PdM, CM, refurbishment, or replacement. Decision standards will be embedded into NEXGEN (job plans, priority rules, approval thresholds) so choices are consistent and traceable. Where trade-offs exist (cost vs. risk vs. performance), we’ll document the rationale and the trigger to revisit.

IV. APPROACH 

**3.2.4. Outcomes of an Integrated Reliability-focused Maintenance Program** Our integrated, reliability-focused maintenance program will align planning, execution, and reliability analysis into a single, transparent process that drives better decisions, reduces risk, and supports the SAMP, capital planning, and improved long-term reliability. Key items for success include: 

- **Connecting Data Across Systems:** Integrating linear asset GIS and inspection history so tunnel and collection system risks are visible alongside plant and vertical process assets. 

- **Standardizing Asset Information:** Enforcing naming conventions, hierarchy standards, and required fields in NEXGEN to ensure consistency and accuracy. 

- **Leveraging Operational Insights:** Mapping SCADA tags to assets to capture runtime, starts, and available condition indicators such as vibration and temperature. 

- **Defining Evidence Standards:** Establishing clear photo documentation and meter point requirements for each asset class to validate work quality and compliance. 

- **Partnering for Success:** Working closely with MMSD as a key partner to ensure alignment, transparency, and shared accountability across all reliability initiatives. 

- **Backlog Discipline and Transparency:** Managing CM/PM backlog as a leading risk indicator—using NEXGEN and dashboards to track and report aging, priority, and resourcing—so critical work is executed before it becomes deferred maintenance that’s not completed until the equipment fails. 

###### **3.3. Reliability Enhancement Plans: Improving Equipment Uptime at JIWRF and SSWRF** 

Based on our review of MMSD’s O&M manuals and the RFP and scope requirements, Jacobs’ maintenance team has already developed initial WRFspecific reliability enhancement plans focused on the equipment and systems that most directly drive uptime at JIWRF and SSWRF. These plans apply the same integrated maintenance backbone described above (standardized asset data, evidence-based work execution, and reliability analytics) to deliver measurable increases in availability, fewer repeat failures, and stronger operational stability. The result is enhanced reliability from more predictable performance and fewer unplanned disruptions caused by nuisance alarms and unexpected equipment shutdowns. At the same time, it will lead to better prioritization of maintenance labor and spares, and clearer performance visibility for MMSD.

**3.3.1. JIWRF Cogeneration Reliability and O&M Enhancement Plan** We can increase equipment availability by reducing nuisance alarms, preventing unplanned equipment shutdowns, and converting priority PM work to conditiondriven actions across the fuel gas conditioning and compression system, cooling water, fuel quality, turbine operating envelope, and waste heat systems. As illustrated in **Exhibit IV-46** , our plan focuses on three practical levers: (1) make data actionable, (2) make alarms meaningful, and (3) prevent repeat shutdowns through disciplined root cause analysis and closure. 

**Actionable reliability data flow:** We’ll connect process signals and equipment condition indicators into a consistent flow from the control system and historian into reliability analytics, then into the CMMS and dashboards, so abnormal conditions trigger the right response work order, and followthrough (not just alarms). 

**Unified alarm strategy and alarm hygiene:** We’ll tier alarms (information, warning, critical, and safety shutdown) with clear expectations for response and escalation. To do that, we’ll evaluate and optimize the alarm system so operators only see alarms that matter, without weakening the safety and equipmentprotection shutdowns that are there to prevent damage or unsafe conditions.

###### **3.5. CM and Work Order Management** 

We won’t just schedule PMs—we’ll optimize them with data and field feedback to maximize uptime and value: 

We won’t just fix failures—we’ll prevent recurrence by embedding root-cause analysis in every corrective work order: 

   - All corrective work will be tracked by Level A/B per the Agreement/Exhibits and managed to MMSD approval thresholds. 

- **PMO.** We’ll run a **formal PMO cycle on critical assets at least annually** . The PMO reviews MTBF/MTTR, PM compliance, failure modes, criticality, and cost, and it examines the task language with frontline staff to remove ambiguity. Each job plan will clearly state the scope, lockout points, steps, tools, acceptance criteria, evidence required (e.g., photo of replaced part), and the trigger for escalation to CM or PdM. PM frequencies will be adjusted by asset class and performance. Changes are vetted through the Maintenance/CMMS Governance Committees to ensure traceability. 

- Each CM work order will capture the failure mode, root cause, corrective action, and preventive measure (“what we’ll do so it doesn’t happen again”). 

- Supervisors will enforce closeout discipline and data quality; overdue or incomplete closeouts trigger follow-ups visible on shared dashboards. 

###### **In-House Failure Analysis Expertise – From Root Cause to Lasting Fixes** 

- **Operator-driven work requests.** We’ll equip operators with NEXGEN’s mobile platform and simple inspection checklists to detect abnormal conditions (smell, sound, temperature, vibration) before failure. 

We’ll bring our failure analysis expertise that includes Jacobs’ O&M regional and heavy maintenance teams, combined with our deep wastewater consulting bench to investigate complex events and train crews to prevent recurrence. Our internal annual audits and monthly cross project CMMS reporting add QC and benchmarking, so performance gaps are surfaced and corrected quickly. 

- **Kitting and parts readiness.** The Planner/Scheduler will coordinate with the warehouse to kit common PMs and align lead-time parts with the look-ahead schedule so crews are never waiting on materials. Inventory staff will be trained in AM principles and participate in the weekly planning meeting.

###### **3.6. Fleet Maintenance** 

Fleet maintenance represents an additional skillset to the wastewater and D&D facilities maintenance, and our approach will ensure all MMSD vehicles and equipment remain reliable, safe, and fully available to support operations across the WRFs, conveyance system, and field environments. We’ll leverage Jacobs’ national fleet management expertise, our CMMS/asset management discipline, and a local team deeply familiar with MMSD’s operating conditions to deliver the following: 

� **Comprehensive Coverage of MMSD’s Fleet:** Jacobs will maintain all vehicles and equipment listed in RFP Exhibit A. Our Fleet Maintenance staff will perform all required PM, approved PdM, and CM activities consistent with RFP Exhibit B requirements, covering on-road vehicles (sedans, pickups, vans, utility vehicles); heavy equipment (front-end loaders, forklifts, man-lifts); sewer maintenance vehicles (vacuum trucks, CCTV trucks); and plant vehicles (burden carriers, bicycles). This includes full compliance with MMSD requirements for vehicle cleanliness, safety equipment, warranty coordination, and NEXGEN CMMS-based documentation as discussed above. 

� **CMMS-Driven, Condition-Based Fleet Management:** Based on our experience implementing NEXGEN, we understand its capabilities and intend to use them, as appropriate, as the backbone of our Fleet Maintenance program. We’ll use NEXGEN’s Asset Inventory module to maintain a complete record for each vehicle—specifications, work order history, and associated PM schedules—all in one place. PM and PdM modules will drive smart scheduling of work orders based on time, meter readings, and condition so we can address emerging issues before they become breakdowns. All work orders—PM, CM, PdM, inspections, and repairs—will be created and closed in real time via mobile devices, with cost, labor, and parts tracked at the asset level to support transparent decisions and optimized lifecycle cost. Using NEXGEN’s AM Planning module, we’ll turn this condition and cost data into forward-looking forecasts of capital and O&M needs, including visuals of expected renewal timing based on useful life, financial life, and inflation assumptions, so MMSD can clearly see fleet reliability risks and replacement priorities. 

� **Reliable PM and CM:** Jacobs will implement a structured PM program that’s integrated with the facilities PM systems. For CM activities, we’ll self-perform repairs wherever possible to minimize downtime, obtain MMSD pre-authorization for outsourced repairs or parts exceeding service agreement thresholds, and maintain spare parts inventory consistent with the Initial Inventory requirements. Additionally, we’ll provide MMSD with a temporary vehicle whenever an MMSD vehicle requires more than1 day of service, ensuring uninterrupted field operations.

###### **3.7. Transparency, Reporting, and Governance** 

We’ll proactively establish the required **Maintenance Committee with two subcommittees—CMMS Governance and SCADA Governance—to co-manage standards, integrations, configuration, and change control.** These bodies are the forum for PM list changes, PMO results, and cross-functional decisions connecting maintenance to capital planning. 

MMSD will have **live dashboard access to PM compliance, CM backlog, work-order aging, labor/cost, asset availability, and risk** . We’ll clearly delineate MMSD-funded vs. Jacobs-funded work under the Agreement’s threshold model, and we’ll **review Standards of Performance** (e.g., on-time PM completion, closeout lag, MTBF/MTTR, availability) **at set cadences with documented actions and owners** . 

**Specific to fleet maintenance** , Jacobs will maintain open, frequent communication with MMSD regarding vehicle repair status and availability, any outsourced repair recommendations, warranty claims and manufacturer coordination, fleet disposal preparation and recommendations, and any operational risks affecting safety, mobility, or mission-critical service. Our approach ensures MMSD’s leadership, governance committees, and operational teams will always have visibility into fleet condition, risks, and costs just as they will with the facilities.

###### **3.8. Staffing, Coverage, and Special Maintenance Functions** 

###### **3.9. Capital Project and Asset Life-Cycle Integration** 

We recognize that the Minimum Staffing Plan in RFP Exhibit E is structured to ensure the vast majority of PM and CM is self-performed. Our staffing approach, detailed in **_Section II – Statement on Staffing, Training, and Corporate Culture_** , is to not just fill positions—we’ll staff to outcomes with a Planner/Scheduler model, CMMS champions, and cross-trained crews for 24/7 reliability. 

We’ll structure shifts/hours for complete coverage, including capital project support, routine and special requests, wet-weather events, and emergencies. Because the CBA currently directs maintenance shifts in an 8-hour day, Monday through Friday structure, we have followed that structure in our proposed shift schedule. 

In addition to this typical shift schedule, we’ll designate staff who will be on standby/on-call to address emergency, wet weather, and related situations to ensure complete coverage as needed. Additionally, we recognize that implementing a shift schedule with 7-days-per-week scheduling may be of interest to MMSD, and we’ll explore this option with MMSD and the unions if appropriate. 

Our approach is a core of self-performing maintenance crews, supported by a dedicated Planner/Scheduler team. Industry baseline is roughly one planner per 20 craft; with NEXGEN and disciplined routines we intend to trend toward a 1:30–1:40 ratio as the system stabilizes. Each crew will designate a CMMS Champion responsible for field data quality and photo evidence. Separate WRF and Conveyance teams will coordinate through weekly planning meetings to balance resources and backlog. 

We’ll track housekeeping, grounds, coatings, tunnel cleaning, and fleet as preventive tasks in the CMMS.  Jacobs’ good-housekeeping standards will be embedded in job plans. Well use subcontractors (e.g., specialty deep-cleaning/ coatings) when it is clearly more efficient, but plan, inspect, and close out subcontractor work through the same workflow and governance. 

Jacobs’ data driven CIP approach leverages asset data collected during maintenance activities to optimize performance, reduce risk and ensure asset reliability. MMSD’s manages hundreds of asset types across its diverse facilities with various operating environments. By developing asset-type specific likelihood of failure factors for the various operating conditions, Jacobs can quantify risk reduction. We’ll apply proven methods, including cost-benefit analysis, multicriteria evaluation and risk-based planning, to balance performance, risk and cost effectively within a CIP. 

A deep understanding of asset level data and its system-wide impacts lead to development of projects that deliver measurable value. With MMSD’s CIP exceeding $2B, as shown in **Exhibit IV-47** , there are many competing priorities and ongoing projects. It’s critical to maintain visibility into current investments and future planned projects to ensure strategic alignment and efficient resource allocation.

Jacobs’ CIP process is an integral part of our asset management program, focusing on maximizing asset life through strategic maintenance, rehabilitation, and renewal. The goal of our CIP process is to drive effective, defensible decisionmaking. We use methodologies such as benefit–cost analysis, multi-criteria analysis, and risk-based decision-making to balance performance, risk, and cost, delivering optimal outcomes in terms of community satisfaction, asset reliability, and financial sustainability.

Our CIP process focuses on three improvement and investment streams: 

Working with MMSD project managers and third-party engineers, our Capital Project Coordinators will: 

- **Asset Expansion:** Upgrading, creating, or purchasing new assets based on growth or elevated service levels. 

   - Support the use of the AMS to identify common assets or adjacent assets that will likely need replacement shortly after a planned capital improvement, especially where there is the potential to save money in the long run. 

- **Asset Renewal:** Upgrading existing assets to maintain required service levels and manage risk. 

- **Strategic Investment:** Providing financial returns, resiliency, or future opportunities. 

New-build CIP initiatives focus on bridging service level gaps and accommodating growth, while ensuring investments consider full lifecycle costs and MMSD’s funding capabilities for operations, maintenance, and asset renewal. Our asset renewal forecasting approach incorporates historic data analysis, lifecycle estimates, predictive modeling, and direct asset observation to prioritize funding effectively. Maintenance data will flow into risk ranking and obsolescence tracking to identify assets approaching end-of-life or due for upgrade. The output is a living reinvestment list ranked by risk, consequence, and lifecycle costs so MMSD can allocate funds where reliability per dollar is maximized.

###### **ABOVE AND BEYOND: MMSD-Specific AI Assistant** 

**AI-assisted knowledge search.** Jacobs’ Intelligent O&M includes a secure, facility-specific assistant, shown in **Exhibit IV-49** , to retrieve procedures, manuals and drawings quickly. This chat-style interface is loaded with plant specific documents only and is NOT accessible to the internet, thereby eliminating responses that are not applicable to MMSD facilities. Through our partnership with advanced AI companies, we leverage the latest AI large language models (Claude, Chat GPT 5, etc.) but in a completely isolated ecosystem that adheres to Jacobs’ AI security data policy and is specific to each client site, without compromising confidentiality. We’ll also review them with MMSD to ensure they meet your data security requirements. We’ve successfully used these tools for two years at our facilities for: 

- Maintenance management (looking up specific equipment information and loading CMMS with PM procedures) 

- Construction management (RFI and construction document search) 

� Contract management (typically restricted to managers only who use them for timely access to contract requirements) 

The tools provide rapid and succinct answers to questions and provide links to the documents that it sourced its answer from. The links/source of information is a critical feature we include to build trust and verify results. Like all our Intelligent O&M tools, it’s intended to support and aid our O&M staff. In this case, it helps them be more efficient and spend less time searching for answers that are tucked away in thousands upon thousands of pages of documents scattered in libraries, offices, SharePoint folders and hard drives. 

This is a signature feature for Jacobs—valued at over $50K/year including licensing and setup. We've refined this tool so well that it is now included in our maintenance package, as a built-in value add for this contract.

###### 4. SAFETY, SECURITY APPROACH AND EMERGENCY PREPAREDNESS 

###### **4.1. Safety** 

###### **4.1.1. Safety First: A Culture of Accountability and Prevention** 

Jacobs will bring MMSD a safety program built on personal accountability, strong leadership, and a commitment to preventing injuries and incidents. Our BeyondZero® philosophy guides day-to-day decisions, reinforces individual responsibility, and promotes a work environment where every employee is empowered to identify hazards and act before they cause harm. Based on our site familiarity, MMSD’s facilities are generally well maintained and clean, providing a strong foundation on which to further reinforce consistent safety practices. At MMSD’s facilities, this approach will translate into safe and reliable operations for staff supporting wastewater facilities, maintenance activities, laboratory functions, solids handling, and capital project interfaces. Our objective is clear: **zero injuries, zero incidents, and zero harm to people, property, or the environment** .
